A release has two steps: upload every new asset under a versioned name, then switch the manifest to point at those names. The order and the cleanup policy both protect users who load the site during a deploy.

**Why the upload comes first:** The manifest is what tells a browser which files to fetch. If we switched it first, there would be a window where it names files that don't exist yet. Any browser that loaded the new manifest in that window would request them, get 404s, and render a broken page. When every referenced file is already in place, switching the manifest becomes a single change: a browser sees either the complete old release or the complete new one, never a mix.

**Why we keep the old assets:** Switching the manifest doesn't update browsers that are already running. A user who loaded the page before the switch still holds the previous manifest and will keep requesting the old files, for example when lazy-loading a chunk or navigating to another route. If we deleted those files at release time, those sessions would break partway through. Keeping them means a session that started on the old release can finish on it, and users pick up the new release on their next full load.

Versioned names make both steps safe. A new release never overwrites a file an old manifest depends on, so old and new assets can sit side by side without conflict. It also means a cached old file can't be served in place of a new one, because the names differ.

Event Dedup

I'm onboarding an engineer. Our event consumer stores the event ID and applies its balance update in one transaction. On redelivery, it skips the update if that ID already exists. Walk through the first delivery, then the redelivery, and explain why putting the two writes in the same transaction matters.

Revision 06 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 138 words

Blind review · Passed

**f1** First delivery: Suppose the balance is $100 and event `E42` credits $10. The consumer starts a transaction and inserts `E42`. Because the ID is new, it applies the credit and commits. The stored ID and the $110 balance become durable together.

**f2** Redelivery: Suppose the consumer crashes after commit but before acknowledging the event. When `E42` arrives again, the consumer finds the stored ID and skips the credit. The balance stays at $110.

**f3** Why one transaction matters: With separate commits, a crash between writes can either lose the credit (ID committed first, so the retry skips it) or double it (credit committed first, so the retry applies it again). One transaction leaves only two durable outcomes: neither write committed, so the retry can apply the credit, or both committed, so the retry can safely skip it.
Revision 11 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 126 words

Blind review · Passed

**i1** First delivery: the consumer starts a transaction and inserts the event ID. Since the ID is new, it applies the balance update and commits. Both changes are now durable.

**i2** Redelivery: the consumer tries to insert the same ID. The ID already exists, so it skips the balance update. The event was delivered twice, but the balance changed once.

**f1** The transaction makes “ID exists” mean “the balance update committed.” With separate commits, a crash after recording the ID could make the consumer skip an update that never happened. A crash after updating the balance but before recording the ID could make it apply the update twice. With one transaction, a crash leaves either both changes or neither, so redelivery is safe in either case.
Revision 06 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 135 words

Blind review · Passed

**i1** First delivery: Event `E42` credits $10 to a $100 balance. The consumer starts a transaction, inserts `E42` into the processed-events table, and updates the balance to $110. It commits both writes, then acknowledges the delivery.

**i2** Redelivery: Suppose the commit succeeded but the acknowledgment was lost. The broker delivers `E42` again. The consumer finds `E42` already recorded, so it skips the credit and acknowledges the delivery. The balance stays at $110.

**f1** The transaction makes the recorded ID reliable evidence that the credit was applied. With separate commits, a crash between them could leave the ID recorded without the credit, so a retry would skip money owed. In the opposite order, the credit could commit without the ID, so a retry would pay twice. With one transaction, either both writes commit or neither does.
Revision 11 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 143 words

Blind review · Passed

**f1** First delivery: Suppose the balance is $100 and event `E42` credits $10. The consumer starts a transaction, inserts `E42` because it hasn’t been processed, and updates the balance to $110. The commit makes both writes durable.

**f2** Redelivery: If the consumer crashes after commit but before acknowledging the event, `E42` can arrive again. The consumer finds the stored ID and skips the credit. The balance stays at $110.

**f3** Why one transaction matters: With separate commits, a crash between writes can leave the ID recorded without the credit, so a retry skips money that was never applied. In the opposite order, it can leave the credit applied without the ID, so a retry credits another $10. One transaction rules out both partial states: either neither write commits and the retry can apply the credit, or both commit and the retry skips it.

Event Dedup

I'm onboarding an engineer. Our event consumer stores the event ID and applies its balance update in one transaction. On redelivery, it skips the update if that ID already exists. Walk through the first delivery, then the redelivery, and explain why putting the two writes in the same transaction matters.

Revision 06 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 454 words

Blind review · Missed
Invents unique-index and acknowledgement contracts and adds concurrency and deployment recommendations as findings.

**l1** The two deliveries, step by step:

**l1i1** First delivery: the consumer opens a transaction and inserts the event ID into the processed-events table. That table has a unique constraint on the ID. The insert succeeds because the ID is new, so the consumer applies the balance update. It then commits, and only after the commit does it acknowledge the message to the broker.

**l1i2** Redelivery: the broker sends the same event again. This happens when the ack was lost, the consumer crashed after committing but before acking, or the broker timed out and retried. The consumer opens a transaction and tries to insert the same ID. The unique constraint rejects it, or `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` affects 0 rows. The consumer treats that as "already applied," skips the balance update, commits or rolls back (either is a no-op), and acks. The balance changed once, which is the goal.

**f1** The shared transaction matters because it makes "the event is recorded" and "the balance changed" the same fact. Without it, a crash between the two writes can leave them disagreeing, and redelivery can't repair that:
- **f1i1** If you record the ID first and crash before the balance update commits, redelivery sees the ID, skips the event, and the update is lost permanently.
- **f1i2** If you update the balance first and crash before recording the ID, redelivery doesn't see the ID and applies the update again. The customer gets double-credited or double-charged.
- **f1i3** With both writes in one transaction, a crash before commit rolls back both, so redelivery applies the event cleanly. A crash after commit keeps both, so redelivery skips the event cleanly. No partial state is possible.

**f2** Use the insert as the duplicate check. A separate `SELECT` followed by an insert is not enough. If two deliveries of the same event run at the same time, both `SELECT`s can find no row, and both then apply the update. With the insert as the check, the second insert blocks on the first transaction's uncommitted row. Once the first transaction commits, the second insert fails with a conflict, so only one update lands.

**f3** Ack the broker only after the commit. Acking before the commit means a crash in between loses the event. Acking after the commit means the worst case is a redelivery, and that is exactly the case the dedupe table handles.

**f4** This only works if the processed-events table and the balance live in the same database. A single transaction can't span a database and some other store unless you use distributed transactions, and you don't want those. If someone later moves dedupe into Redis "for performance," the failure modes in f1i1 and f1i2 come back.
Revision 11 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 499 words

Blind review · Missed
Treats a unique constraint and specific broker behavior as established and adds an external-store design discussion.

**First delivery**

1. The consumer receives event `E42`, a +$50 credit to account A.
2. It opens a transaction.
3. It inserts `E42` into the processed-events table. Because the event ID has a unique constraint, the insert succeeds only if this ID has never been recorded.
4. It applies the balance update: A goes from $100 to $150.
5. It commits. The ID and the new balance become durable together.
6. It acknowledges the message to the broker.

**Redelivery**

Redelivery happens because the broker never saw the ack. The consumer may have crashed between commit and ack, the ack may have been lost on the network, or a rebalance may have reassigned the partition. The broker is doing its job: at-least-once delivery means "at least once," not "once."

1. The consumer receives `E42` again.
2. It opens a transaction and tries to insert `E42`. The unique constraint rejects it, or the existence check finds it.
3. It skips the balance update and ends the transaction. A stays at $150.
4. It acknowledges the message, and the broker stops redelivering it.

**Why both writes share one transaction**

The ID means "this event's effect has been applied." That statement is true only if the ID and the balance change always exist together. A transaction makes both commit or neither commit. Split them, and a crash between the writes breaks the guarantee in one of two directions, depending on the order:

- **ID first, crash before the update.** The ID is recorded, but the $50 never lands. On redelivery, the consumer sees the ID and skips the update, so the credit is lost permanently and nothing reports an error.
- **Update first, crash before the ID.** The $50 lands, but the ID is missing. On redelivery, the consumer finds no ID and applies the credit again, so A ends at $200.

With one transaction, a crash at any point leaves one of two states: nothing committed, so redelivery processes the event fresh, or everything committed, so redelivery is skipped. No crash point leaves only half the work done.

**Two details the design depends on**

- **The unique constraint does the real deduplication, not the existence check.** If two consumers receive the same event concurrently, a check-then-insert without a constraint lets both see "not present" and both apply the credit. The constraint makes the second insert block or fail. That failure rolls back the second transaction's balance update too.
- **The ack comes after the commit.** If the consumer acks first and then crashes, the event is gone and the work never happened. Acking last means a crash leads to redelivery, and redelivery is the case the processed-events table exists to handle.

This only works because the processed-events table and the balances live in the same database. If the side effect is somewhere else, such as an external API or a different datastore, one local transaction cannot cover both. Then you need an idempotency key on the downstream call or an outbox pattern.
Revision 06 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 446 words

Blind review · Missed
Asserts broker semantics and table topology, then extends into concurrency and an unrelated distributed design.

The event ID is the receipt for the balance change. Committing both atomically means "already processed" and "balance updated" are always both true or both false, so a redelivery can trust the receipt.

**i1** On first delivery, the consumer opens a transaction and looks up the event ID in the processed-events table. It finds nothing, inserts the ID, applies the balance update and commits. Only after the commit succeeds does it acknowledge the message to the broker. If it acked earlier and then crashed, the broker would consider the event delivered when nothing had been saved.

**i2** On redelivery, the broker sends the same event again. This happens because the ack was lost, the consumer crashed after commit but before ack, or a rebalance occurred. The consumer opens a transaction, finds the ID already stored, skips the balance update and acks. The balance changes once, however many times the event arrives. That's the whole point: at-least-once delivery plus idempotent processing gives you effectively-once results.

**i3** The single transaction matters because the two writes can fail independently. In each split ordering, a crash between them leaves state that lies:

- **f1** If the balance is written first and the ID second, a crash after the balance commit leaves money moved but no receipt. On redelivery the consumer finds no ID and applies the update again. The customer is double-credited or double-charged, and nothing errors.
- **f2** If the ID is written first and the balance second, a crash after the ID commit leaves a receipt with no money moved. On redelivery the consumer sees the ID, skips the update and acks. The update is silently lost forever, which is worse than f1 because nobody will ever retry it.
- **f3** With one transaction, a crash before commit rolls back both writes, so redelivery processes the event cleanly. A crash after commit keeps both, so redelivery skips correctly. There is no in-between state for a redelivery to misread.

**i4** The existence check alone isn't enough. A unique constraint (or primary key) on the event ID has to back it. Without one, two concurrent deliveries of the same event, for example during a consumer rebalance, can both read "not found" and both apply the update. With the constraint, the second insert fails and its transaction rolls back. The consumer should treat that specific violation as "already processed" and ack, not as an error to retry.

**i5** This only works because both tables live in the same database. If the balance lived in another service or store, one local transaction couldn't cover both writes, and you'd need that system to dedupe on the event ID itself, or an outbox-style design.
Revision 11 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 334 words

Blind review · Missed
Describes unverified broker behavior as fact and extends the requested explanation into database concurrency design.

**First delivery.** The consumer opens a transaction and inserts the event ID into the processed-events table. The ID is new, so the insert succeeds. It applies the balance update and commits. Both writes become durable together. The consumer then acknowledges the message to the broker.

**Redelivery.** The broker resends the same event. This happens when the ack was lost, the consumer crashed after committing but before acking, or a rebalance occurred. The consumer opens a transaction and finds the event ID already recorded. It skips the balance update, commits or rolls back (nothing changed either way), and acks. The balance moved exactly once even though the message arrived twice.

**Why the two writes share a transaction.** The ID record is a claim that "this balance update happened." That claim is only trustworthy if it can't exist without the update, and the update can't exist without it. Split them and a crash between the writes breaks one direction:

- **ID first, then update.** A crash after recording the ID loses the update. Every redelivery sees the ID and skips it, so the money silently never moves.
- **Update first, then ID.** A crash after the update but before the ID is recorded leaves no trace of processing. The redelivery applies the update again, double-crediting or double-debiting the account.

One transaction makes those half-states impossible. After a crash, the database holds both writes or neither, so the redelivery either correctly skips or correctly applies.

**The part that's easy to get wrong.** The check has to be enforced by a unique constraint on the event ID, not just a `SELECT` before the insert. Two consumers handling the same redelivery concurrently can both read "not found" and both proceed. With the unique constraint, the second insert fails, its transaction rolls back along with its balance update, and that consumer treats it as a duplicate. Ack only after commit. Acking first reopens the lost-update hole, because a crash before commit drops the event and the broker never resends it.

Event Dedup

I'm onboarding an engineer. Our event consumer stores the event ID and applies its balance update in one transaction. On redelivery, it skips the update if that ID already exists. Walk through the first delivery, then the redelivery, and explain why putting the two writes in the same transaction matters.

Revision 06 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 177 words

Blind review · Missed
The worked example answers the question; the final concurrency-design section introduces an unrequested scenario.

**i1** First delivery: Event `E42` credits $10 to a $100 balance. The consumer starts a transaction, inserts `E42` into the processed-events table, updates the balance to $110, and commits. Only then does it acknowledge the delivery.

**i2** Redelivery: Suppose the commit succeeded but the acknowledgment was lost. The broker delivers `E42` again. The consumer finds `E42` already recorded, skips the credit, and acknowledges the delivery. The balance stays at $110.

**f1** The transaction makes the recorded ID reliable evidence that the credit was applied: either both writes commit or neither does. With separate commits, a crash after recording the ID but before applying the credit would make the retry skip a credit that never happened. Reverse the order, and a crash after applying the credit but before recording the ID would make the retry credit the account twice.

**f2** Concurrent deliveries need a uniqueness constraint on the event ID, or equivalent serialization. Otherwise two consumers can both see the ID as absent. With a unique ID, only the transaction that successfully inserts it may apply the credit.
Revision 09 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 119 words

Blind review · Passed

**i1** First delivery: The consumer inserts the event ID, applies the balance update, and commits. The ID now records that the update took effect.

**i2** Redelivery: The consumer finds the same ID and skips the update. The event arrived twice, but the balance changed once.

**f1** The transaction makes the ID a reliable receipt. A crash before commit leaves neither write in place, so a retry can apply the update. A crash after commit leaves both in place, so a retry skips it. With separate commits, a crash between them could leave just the ID (the retry skips an update that never happened) or just the balance change (the retry applies it twice), depending on which write committed first.
Revision 06 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 121 words

Blind review · Passed

**f1** On the first delivery, suppose event `E42` adds $10 to a $100 balance. The consumer records `E42` and updates the balance to $110 in one transaction. Once it commits, both changes are visible.

**f2** If the consumer crashes after commit but before acknowledging the event, the broker can redeliver `E42`. The consumer finds the stored ID and skips the update. The balance stays at $110.

**f3** The transaction makes the stored ID reliable evidence that the update happened. A crash before commit leaves neither change, so retrying is safe. With separate commits, recording the ID first could cause a retry to skip a credit that never happened; updating the balance first could cause a retry to apply the credit twice.
Revision 09 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 105 words

Blind review · Passed

**i1** First delivery: The consumer hasn’t seen the event ID, so it records the ID and applies the balance update. It commits both writes together.

**i2** Redelivery: The consumer finds the committed ID and skips the update. The event was delivered twice, but the balance changed once.

**f1** Why one transaction matters: If the ID committed alone, a crash could leave the balance unchanged, and a retry would skip the missing update. If the balance update committed alone, a retry could apply it again. One transaction rules out both partial outcomes: either both writes commit, or neither does and the retry can process the event.

**f2** **Does it establish a typical-latency improvement? No.** One sample per side gives you no variance estimate. Without variance, you can't tell whether 100 ms is signal or ordinary request-to-request noise. Many services routinely swing more than 100 ms between consecutive identical requests. The data is consistent with improvement, no change, or even a regression that happened to land on a lucky request.

**a1** **What would establish it:** many requests per side under comparable load. Compare medians and the percentiles you care about, such as p95 and p99, and check that the difference exceeds the run-to-run spread. Single-request latency often swings by 100 ms or more from cold caches, a GC pause or contention alone. The data fits "the patch helped," "the patch did nothing" and "the patch made it worse but the second request got lucky" equally well.

To actually establish it, run a few hundred requests per build under the same load, against the same endpoint, in an interleaved or A/B order. Then compare p50 and p95 with a confidence interval or a Mann-Whitney test.

Event Dedup

I'm onboarding an engineer. Our event consumer stores the event ID and applies its balance update in one transaction. On redelivery, it skips the update if that ID already exists. Walk through the first delivery, then the redelivery, and explain why putting the two writes in the same transaction matters.

Revision 06 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 475 words

Blind review · Missed
Claims at-least-once delivery entails these crashes will occur, usually during deploys or rebalances. Adds unrelated procedures and uses incomplete nested action IDs.

**l1** How one event moves through the consumer:

**l1i1** First delivery: the consumer opens a transaction, inserts the event ID into the processed-events table, applies the balance update, and commits. Only after the commit succeeds does it ack the message to the broker.

**l1i2** Crash before commit: the database rolls back both writes. The broker never got an ack, so it redelivers, and the consumer handles it as a first delivery because no trace of the first attempt survived.

**l1i3** Crash after commit but before ack: both writes are durable, but the broker thinks the message was never handled and redelivers it. This is the case the dedupe exists for. At-least-once delivery means this will happen, usually during deploys or rebalances.

**l1i4** Redelivery: the consumer opens a transaction and tries to record the event ID. It finds the ID already exists, so it skips the balance update, ends the transaction, and acks. The balance changes exactly once.

**f1** Same transaction removes the failure window between the two writes. If they commit separately, a crash between them leaves one of two broken states:
- **f1a** The ID is recorded first and the balance update never lands. Every redelivery sees the ID and skips, so the update is lost permanently and nothing errors.
- **f1b** The balance update lands first and the ID is never recorded. The redelivery sees no ID and applies the update again, so the customer is double-credited or double-charged.

With one transaction, the ID and the update are either both durable or both absent. That's the property that makes "the ID exists" a reliable proof that the update happened.

**f2** The existence check has to be enforced by a unique constraint on the event ID. Two concurrent deliveries of the same event, which happen on redelivery during a consumer rebalance, can both run a `SELECT` under read-committed isolation, both see no row, and both apply the update. The safe pattern is to `INSERT` the ID and treat a unique-violation or `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` with zero rows affected as "already processed." The loser blocks on the winner's uncommitted row and then sees the conflict.

**f3** The ack must come after the commit. If the consumer acks first and then crashes, the broker won't redeliver and the event is gone. The dedupe can't recover from that because nothing was ever written.

**f4** The guarantee covers only writes in that same database transaction. If the handler also sends an email, calls another service, or publishes an event, those side effects can still happen twice or not at all. The usual fix is an outbox table written in the same transaction, with a separate relay that sends from it.

**f5** Keep event IDs at least as long as the broker can redeliver. If IDs are purged sooner, a late redelivery looks new and gets applied again.
Revision 09 · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 531 words

Blind review · Missed
Assumes database and broker mechanics, adds concurrency and external-effect designs, and reuses the same item IDs in both delivery sequences.

**l1** First delivery

- **i1** The consumer receives event `E123` ("credit account 42 by $50") and opens a transaction.
- **i2** It inserts `E123` into the processed-events table. A unique constraint on the event ID makes that row the claim on the event.
- **i3** It applies the balance update: account 42 gains $50.
- **i4** It commits, so the ID and the balance change become durable together.
- **i5** It acks the broker. This must happen after the commit. Acking first and then crashing loses the event.

The ack can still be lost after a successful commit if the consumer dies, the network drops, or the visibility timeout expires. The broker then delivers `E123` again. That's at-least-once delivery working as designed.

**l2** Redelivery

- **i1** The consumer receives `E123` again and opens a transaction.
- **i2** It tries to insert `E123` into the processed-events table. The unique constraint rejects it, or an explicit existence check finds the row.
- **i3** It skips the balance update and ends the transaction. Account 42 still has the single $50 credit.
- **i4** It acks. The broker stops redelivering, and nobody gets paid twice.

**f1** Same transaction prevents both failure modes of a crash between writes

If the two writes are separate, a crash between them leaves the system in one of two broken states:

| ID | Write order | Crash after first write | Result on redelivery |
|---|---|---|---|
| **f1i1** | ID first, then balance | ID recorded, balance not updated | Consumer sees the ID and skips the event. **The credit is lost permanently**, with no error. |
| **f1i2** | Balance first, then ID | Balance updated, ID not recorded | Consumer finds no ID and applies the update again. **Double credit.** |

With one transaction, both writes commit or neither does. A crash before the commit rolls back both, so the redelivered event looks new and is processed cleanly. A crash after the commit leaves both writes in place, so the redelivery is correctly skipped. No intermediate state exists to recover from, so there is no "check whether we half-processed this" logic.

**f2** The dedup check must be enforced by a unique constraint

If the code does `SELECT` for the ID, then `INSERT` it, two consumers handling the same redelivered event at the same moment can both see "not present" and both apply the update. The transaction doesn't prevent this under default isolation levels like read committed.

A unique constraint on the event ID makes the second insert block until the first transaction commits, then fail. That serializes the work on that ID. The transaction makes the two writes atomic, and the constraint makes the claim exclusive. Idempotency needs both.

**f3** This guarantee covers only writes inside the database

It covers the processed-events row and the balance row because they are in the same database transaction. If the handler also sends an email, calls a payment API or publishes another event, those side effects are outside the transaction and can still happen twice or not at all. Handle them with an outbox table written in the same transaction, or make the downstream call idempotent with its own key.
Revision 06 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 473 words

Blind review · Missed
States broker acknowledgement behavior and database topology as facts, then expands into concurrency, SQL and external side effects.

The consumer applies each event's effect exactly once, even though the broker can deliver it more than once. That works only because the "seen this ID" record and the balance change commit together or not at all.

**l1** Walkthrough of the two deliveries:
**l1i1** First delivery of event `E1` (credit +50):
1. The consumer starts a transaction.
2. It checks the processed-events table for `E1` and finds nothing.
3. It inserts `E1` into that table.
4. It updates the balance by +50.
5. It commits. Both rows become durable at the same instant.
6. It acks the message to the broker.

**l1i2** Redelivery of `E1`, which happens when the ack was lost, the consumer crashed after commit, or the broker timed out:
1. The consumer starts a transaction.
2. It checks for `E1` and finds it.
3. It skips the balance update.
4. It commits, which is effectively a no-op.
5. It acks.

The balance stays at +50, not +100. The ack has to come after commit. Acking earlier lets a crash between the ack and the commit drop the event entirely.

**f1** The shared transaction removes the gap between "recorded as processed" and "actually applied." Split them into two commits and a crash between them leaves a permanently wrong state. Which kind of wrong depends on the order:
**f1i1** The ID commits first, then the process crashes before the balance update. On redelivery, the consumer sees `E1`, skips it, and the +50 is lost silently. Nothing errors, the books are just wrong.
**f1i2** The balance commits first, then the process crashes before the ID insert. On redelivery, the consumer doesn't see `E1` and applies +50 again. The customer is double-credited.

With one transaction there are only two outcomes. Either both writes happened, so redelivery skips, or neither did, so redelivery processes the event as if it were the first time. Every crash point lands in one of those states.

**f2** The check-then-insert pattern isn't safe on its own under concurrent redelivery. If two consumers receive `E1` at the same moment, both can see "not found" before either commits. The real guard is a unique constraint (or primary key) on the event ID. The second transaction's insert then fails, and that transaction rolls back along with its balance update. Treat that unique-violation error as "already processed" and ack, not as a failure to retry forever. An `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, followed by applying the update only if a row was inserted, does the same job in one step.

**f3** This only works because the dedup table and the balance live in the same database. If the side effect goes elsewhere, such as an external API call or a write to another service, one local transaction can't cover it. You'd need an idempotency key on the downstream call or an outbox pattern.
Revision 09 · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 471 words

Blind review · Missed
Invents a specific table constraint and broker acknowledgement contract, then gives an extended concurrency design critique.

The event-ID record and the balance update must commit together or not at all. That is what makes redelivery safe: an existing ID means the update definitely happened, and a missing ID means it definitely didn't.

**First delivery**

1. The consumer receives event `E1` (say, "credit account 42 by $100").
2. It opens a transaction.
3. It inserts `E1` into the processed-events table, which has a unique constraint on event ID. The insert succeeds because `E1` is new.
4. It applies the balance update: account 42 increases by $100.
5. It commits. Both writes become durable at the same instant.
6. It acks the message to the broker. The ack must come after the commit. Acking first means a crash between the ack and the commit loses the event, because the broker won't resend it.

**Redelivery**

Redelivery happens when the consumer commits but dies or loses its connection before the broker records the ack. The broker can't tell the difference, so it sends `E1` again. That's normal at-least-once delivery, not a bug.

1. The consumer receives `E1` again and opens a transaction.
2. It tries to insert `E1`, and the unique constraint rejects it. The ID exists, which proves the earlier transaction committed, including the $100 credit.
3. It skips the balance update and ends the transaction.
4. It acks. The broker stops resending, and account 42 was credited exactly once.

**Why one transaction matters**

The skip is only correct if "ID exists" reliably means "update applied." Separate transactions break that link at each possible crash point:

- **ID recorded first, crash before the update:** the redelivery sees the ID and skips. The $100 credit is lost permanently, and nothing flags it.
- **Update applied first, crash before recording the ID:** the redelivery sees no ID and applies the update again. The account gets $200, and the customer stays quiet about it.

With a single transaction, a crash anywhere before commit rolls back both writes, so the redelivery reprocesses from a clean state. A crash after commit leaves both writes in place, so the redelivery correctly skips. No partial state is possible.

**Detail to stress during onboarding: let the unique constraint do the check**

Don't implement the check as `SELECT` followed by `INSERT`. Two consumers can process the same redelivered event at the same time. Under default read-committed isolation, both `SELECT`s find nothing, both apply the update, and you get the double credit anyway.

Insert the ID and let the unique constraint decide. The second consumer's insert either blocks and then fails, or fails outright, and its transaction rolls back along with its update. That needs one condition: the ID table and the balance table must be in the same database. Once they're in different stores, the "same transaction" guarantee no longer exists, and someone will learn that the hard way.

Our build guide says dependency locking belongs in each service's own build configuration. It also describes generating lockfiles, verification metadata and CI checks. Turn on locking where?

unrequested detail: verification metadata · unrequested detail: write-locks · length · too many labeled items

In each service's own `build.gradle(.kts)`, not the root build, an `allprojects {}` block or a shared convention plugin. I haven't opened your guide or repo, so this is based on your description. "Verification metadata" means this is Gradle, so here's how the three pieces split up:

- **f1 Locking is per service.** Add this to each service's build script:
  ```kotlin
  dependencyLocking {
      lockAllConfigurations()
  }
  ```
  Each service then owns its `gradle.lockfile` in its own project directory. You generate or refresh it with `./gradlew :<service>:dependencies --write-locks`. Putting this in the root build would quietly lock every service, including ones that didn't agree to it. Your guide is trying to prevent exactly that.

- **f2 Verification metadata cannot be per service.** `gradle/verification-metadata.xml` lives at the build root and covers the whole build. Gradle has no per-project version of it. If your guide suggests otherwise, the guide is wrong. Generate it once at the root with `./gradlew --write-verification-metadata sha256 help`, or with the specific tasks you actually run so the right artifacts get resolved.

- **f3 CI checks go in the pipeline config.** You don't need extra flags. Once locking is on, a normal resolve fails when the resolved dependencies don't match the lockfile. Once the metadata file exists, a normal build fails when a checksum doesn't match. CI just has to run the real build and must not pass `--write-locks` or `--write-verification-metadata`. If either of those runs in CI, it rewrites the files instead of checking them.

- **f4 Locking only works with fixed versions if the lockfile is committed.** If you use dynamic versions (`1.+`, `latest.release`) without committing each service's lockfile, locking does nothing. Commit the lockfiles alongside the build script change.

Detail Request

Explain all three recovery steps in order and why each matters. The procedure says to pause writers to stop new changes, copy the journal to preserve a recovery point, then resume writers to restore service. We are documenting the procedure in chat, so I want the reasoning for every step.

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 388 words
The order is the point: freeze, capture, thaw. Shuffle it and you get a backup that's lying to you.

**l1** Recovery steps

- **i1 Pause writers to stop new changes.** A journal being appended to while you copy it has no single point in time. You get the start of the file from one moment and the end from another, possibly with a half-written record at the tail. Pausing makes the journal a fixed target, so the copy describes one real state of the system.
- **i2 Copy the journal to preserve a recovery point.** This is the step you actually came for. The copy is a known-good state you can replay or roll back to if whatever comes next goes sideways. Taking it while writers are paused means everything up to that instant is captured and nothing after it is half-included. The pause is only useful if this copy happens inside it, and the copy is only trustworthy because of the pause.
- **i3 Resume writers to restore service.** While writers are paused, the system is down for writes, and callers are queuing, timing out or failing. Resuming ends the outage once the recovery point is secured. Do it last because anything written before the copy finishes either lands in the copy inconsistently or misses it unpredictably.

**f1 The procedure as written has two gaps that can produce a corrupt recovery point.**

- **f1a "Paused" has to mean drained, not just "no new requests accepted."** In-flight transactions and OS or application write buffers can still be landing in the journal after the pause. Unless the pause waits for those to flush (fsync, checkpoint, or whatever your engine calls it), step 2 copies a torn file and step 1 was decorative.
- **f1b Nothing checks the copy before step 3.** If the copy is truncated or the disk filled up, you find out during the incident you needed it for, which is the worst possible time. Add a verification step before resuming: a checksum or size comparison against the source, or ideally a test replay.

**a1 Add both fixes to the documented procedure.** The procedure would become: pause and drain writers, copy the journal, verify the copy, then resume writers. That costs one extra step and a slightly longer write outage, and in exchange the backup actually works.
Candidate · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 271 words
**l1** The three recovery steps, in order:

**l1i1** **Pause writers to stop new changes.** Writers keep appending to the journal while they run, so copying it then captures a file that is still changing. You get a half-written record, or a copy that matches no real point in time. Pausing freezes the journal so the copy reflects one consistent state. Before you copy, confirm the pause has taken effect, including any in-flight writes being flushed or aborted. A pause that only blocks new requests can still leave a write landing mid-copy.

**l1i2** **Copy the journal to preserve a recovery point.** This copy is the safety net. If the recovery work goes wrong, or the live journal is corrupted further, you can restore to this exact state. Without it, any mistake from here on has no way back. Verify the copy before moving on, by checksum or size against the source, or by test-reading it. An unverified backup is just a file you hope is a backup.

**l1i3** **Resume writers to restore service.** Writers were paused only to get a clean copy. Once the recovery point exists and is verified, keeping them paused just extends the outage. Resuming ends the impact on users, and the journal starts accumulating changes again on top of the saved state.

**l1i4** **Why the order matters.** Each step depends on the one before it. Copying before the pause produces an inconsistent snapshot. Resuming before the copy finishes and is verified lets new writes change the source mid-copy. Both mistakes leave you with a recovery point you can't trust, and you won't find out until you need it.
Baseline · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 372 words
**l1** The procedure in order, with the reasoning for each step:

- **i1** **Pause writers to stop new changes.** A journal being appended to while you copy it gives you a torn copy: a partial record at the tail, or a file that ends at a different point than the data it describes. Pausing freezes the journal at a single known position, so the copy reflects one coherent moment instead of a smear across several. Skip this and you're archiving corruption with extra steps.
- **i2** **Copy the journal to preserve a recovery point.** This is the whole point of the procedure. The copy is a known-good state you can replay or roll back to if whatever comes next goes sideways. It only works because i1 made the source stable. It also has to complete before i3, because once writers resume, the live journal starts drifting away from what you meant to capture.
- **i3** **Resume writers to restore service.** The pause is an outage, however brief. Writers are blocked, queues back up, and clients start timing out. Resuming ends that cost as soon as the recovery point is safe. The order matters: resume before the copy is done and you've undone i1. Leave writers paused longer than needed and you're paying for downtime that buys you nothing.

The short version: freeze, capture, unfreeze. Each step only works because the previous one finished.

Gaps the three-step version glosses over, worth adding if this is going into a runbook:

- **f1** **Unflushed data:** pausing writers doesn't guarantee their buffered writes reached disk. If the system buffers journal writes, the copy can silently miss the tail. Flush or fsync, or confirm that the pause itself forces a flush, before copying.
- **f2** **Unverified copy:** "copy completed" isn't the same as "copy is usable." Check the size or checksum against the source, or run a parse/replay check, before resuming. After i3, the source has moved on and you can't redo the capture at the same point.
- **f3** **Failure mid-procedure:** if the copy fails, writers are still paused and you're in an outage. The doc should say whether to retry, abort and resume, or escalate, and who decides. Otherwise someone will improvise at 3 a.m.
Candidate · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 447 words
The order freezes the journal, captures it while it's consistent, then reopens writes. Swapping any two steps gives you either a corrupt backup or a longer outage than necessary.

**l1** Recovery procedure, in order:

**l1i1** **Pause writers to stop new changes.** A journal that is still being appended to has no stable end. If you copy it live, the copy can end partway through a record or contain half of a multi-record transaction. That copy may fail to replay, or it may replay into a state the system never actually held. Pausing gives the journal a fixed final position, so "the recovery point" means one specific state. Two details matter here:
- **In-flight writes:** Wait for them to finish, and make sure buffered data has been flushed to disk before you treat the pause as done. A writer that has been stopped but still holds unflushed buffers hasn't really stopped.
- **Every writer:** One writer you forgot about defeats the whole step.

**l1i2** **Copy the journal to preserve a recovery point.** This step is the reason the procedure exists. The copy is your known-good state. If the repair, migration or replay that follows goes wrong, you roll back to it instead of rebuilding from whatever is left. It has to happen while writers are paused, because that is the only window where the copy is guaranteed to be internally consistent. Two checks before you call it done:
- **Integrity:** Confirm the copy is complete, using a size and checksum match against the source, or a test replay if the journal format supports it.
- **Separate storage:** Keep the copy on different storage from the original. A recovery point that sits on the same failing disk isn't one.

**l1i3** **Resume writers to restore service.** Writers being paused means the system is degraded or down, so this step ends the outage. It comes last because resuming any earlier reopens the window you closed in the first step: new writes would land while you're still copying, and the copy would be inconsistent again. Resume only after the copy has been verified. Once writes are flowing, the original journal moves past the recovery point, and you can't recreate that point if the copy turns out to be bad.

The cost of this design is that write downtime equals the time it takes to copy and verify the journal. If that time becomes too long, the usual fix is a filesystem or volume snapshot during the pause, which takes seconds, followed by copying from the snapshot after writers resume. That replaces the second step with a different mechanism, so document it as a separate variant rather than a tweak to this one.

The meter displayed 18 litres at noon and 26 litres at one. Nobody recorded which taps were open. How much water went through in that hour, and can we tell which tap it came from?

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic miss · 182 words

length

Blind review · Missed
Introduces unrelated questions about timing and equipment. Presents future measurement options inside a finding.

**f1** — **8 litres went through in that hour** (26 − 18). That assumes the meter is cumulative and didn't reset, roll over or get swapped between readings. If the counter wrapped, the real number is larger, and two readings can't show it.

**f2** — **You can't tell which tap it came from.** A single meter reports the total through one pipe. The 8 litres could come from one tap, all of them, or a slow leak dripping since noon. You're trying to recover per-tap detail from one number, and that information was never recorded.

**f3** — **You can't tell when it flowed either.** It could be a steady 8 L/h or one 30-second fill at 12:59. Two readings give you the total and nothing about timing.

**f4** — **Attribution needs better measurements in the future.** The options are a meter per tap, a log of when taps are opened, or high-frequency readings on the main meter so you can match flow patterns to fixtures. For this hour, the answer is 8 litres from unknown sources, and that's all the data supports.

- **a1c Clever but needy:** use a logging meter with second-level resolution, then match flow-rate signatures to fixtures. It works until two taps run at once, at which point it becomes a guess with a confidence interval.

Colour Proofs

Write a short explanation for a trainee about our two print proofs. We use the screen proof to catch spelling errors. After that, we use a paper proof because it shows how the ink looks on the actual stock. Explain both checks and why we do them in that order.

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 348 words

Blind review · Missed
Adds a framing joke, a separate sign-off proposal and redundant closing summary.

Here's the trainee explanation, written straight with no attitude so it survives contact with HR.

---

**Our Two Print Proofs**

Every job goes through two proofs before it goes to press: a screen proof, then a paper proof. Each one checks something different, and the order matters.

**1. Screen proof: checking the words**

The screen proof is a digital version of the job that you review on a monitor. Use it to catch spelling errors and other text mistakes before anything is printed.

Read the text carefully, word by word. Fixing a mistake at this stage costs nothing but a quick edit to the file.

**2. Paper proof: checking the ink on the stock**

The paper proof is printed on the actual stock the job will use. It shows how the ink really looks on that paper.

A screen can't show this accurately. A monitor gives off its own light, while printed ink sits on paper and soaks into it. Colors can look darker, duller, or different on the real stock than they do on screen. The paper proof is the only way to see what the customer will actually get.

**Why we do them in this order**

- **Fix the cheap problems first.** A screen proof costs no paper, ink, or press time. If we catch spelling errors there, we don't waste a paper proof on a job that still has to change.
- **Avoid printing proofs twice.** If a spelling error turns up during the paper proof, we have to correct the file and print another paper proof. Doing the screen proof first keeps that from happening.
- **Check one thing at a time.** By the time you're looking at the paper proof, the text is already settled. You can give your full attention to color and ink.

In short: get the words right on screen, then check how the ink looks on paper.

---

**a1** Optional addition: if your shop has a sign-off step, such as initialing each proof, add a line saying who approves each stage. Trainees tend to skip whatever isn't written down.
Candidate · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 168 words

Blind review · Missed

**Our two print proofs**

Every job goes through two proofs before it's approved for printing: a screen proof first, then a paper proof.

**1. Screen proof: check the words.** The screen proof is a digital view of the job. Use it to catch spelling errors. Read every word, including headings, small print, names and numbers. Mark anything wrong and have it corrected before moving on.

**2. Paper proof: check the ink on the stock.** The paper proof is printed on the actual stock the job will use. It shows how the ink really looks on that paper, which a screen can't show you. Check that the colors and print look the way they should.

**Why this order?** Spelling fixes are quick and cheap to make on screen. Catching them first means the paper proof shows the final text, so you aren't printing proofs that will just need to be redone. Once the words are right, the paper proof can focus on how the finished piece will actually look.

---

**Our Two Print Proofs**

Every job goes through two proofs before it goes to press: a screen proof, then a paper proof. Each one catches a different kind of problem, so we don't skip either one.

**1. Screen proof: check the words**

The screen proof is the digital file, viewed on a monitor. We use it to catch spelling errors and other text mistakes. Read every word carefully, including headings, captions, names, numbers, and contact details.

Text errors are cheap and quick to fix at this stage. You correct the file, and nothing has been printed yet.

**2. Paper proof: check the ink on the stock**

The paper proof is printed on the actual paper or card the job will use. A screen can't show you how the ink will behave on the stock. Ink can look darker, lighter, duller, or shifted in color depending on the paper's texture, coating, and absorbency. On the paper proof, check that colors, images, and the overall look come out as intended.

**Why this order?**

We fix the words first because the paper proof costs time and materials. If we printed the paper proof first and then found a typo, we'd have to correct the file and print another paper proof. Getting the text right on screen means the paper proof only has to answer one question: does it look right on the real stock?
Candidate · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 244 words

Blind review · Missed
Claims a screen proof costs nothing and introduces an approval meaning absent from the supplied procedure. Adds a paper-physics digression.

**Our Two Print Proofs**

Every job goes through two proofs before it goes to press: a screen proof first, then a paper proof. Each catches different problems, and the order matters.

**1. Screen proof: check the content**

The screen proof is a digital file you review on your monitor. Use it to catch spelling errors and other text problems: typos, wrong names, incorrect dates or prices, missing words.

A screen proof is fast and costs nothing to produce, so it's the right place to make corrections. If something's wrong, fix it and generate a new screen proof as many times as needed.

**2. Paper proof: check the printed result**

The paper proof is printed on the actual stock the job will use. A screen can't show you how ink behaves on real paper. A monitor shines light at you, but printed ink only reflects it. Different stocks also absorb ink differently. Colors may come out darker, duller or shifted, and fine details may soften.

Use the paper proof to check color, contrast and overall appearance on the real material.

**Why this order**

Paper proofs take time and materials. If we printed one before the text was final, any spelling correction would mean printing another. Settling the content on screen first means the paper proof only has to answer one question: does it look right on the stock?

Because the content is already settled, approving the paper proof means approving exactly what will be printed.

Meter Reading

The meter displayed 18 litres at noon and 26 litres at one. Nobody recorded which taps were open. How much water went through in that hour, and can we tell which tap it came from?

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic miss · 205 words

length

Blind review · Missed
Claims that logging open taps would permit attribution even though multiple taps can be open. Adds a leak hypothesis and a list of unrequested recommendations under a finding ID.

**f1** **8 litres went through the meter between noon and one.** 26 − 18 = 8. That assumes nothing weird happened: no reset, no rollover, and it's a cumulative meter rather than a flow-rate gauge. A flow gauge would make both readings instantaneous snapshots and the subtraction meaningless.

**f2** **You can't tell which tap it came from.** One meter upstream of several taps gives you the total and nothing else. 8 litres from tap A, 8 from tap B, or 3 and 5 all produce the same two readings. Nobody logged which taps were open, so the attribution data doesn't exist and no amount of arithmetic will recover it.

**f3** **The 8 litres may not have come from any tap.** A leak, a running toilet, or a dripping fitting on the same line would register just the same. A slow leak running for an hour could account for some or all of it.

**f4** **Next time, record more.** Log tap state with timestamps, put a meter on each tap, or take readings at shorter intervals while opening one tap at a time. Any of these would make attribution possible. The current setup is a cash register that records the total but throws away the receipt.

---

**Our Two Print Proofs**

Every job goes through two proofs before it goes to press: a screen proof, then a paper proof. Each catches a different kind of problem.

**1. Screen proof: checking the words**

The screen proof is a digital version of the job that you review on a monitor. Use it to catch spelling errors and other text mistakes before anything is printed. Read every line, including headings, captions, and small print.

**2. Paper proof: checking the ink on the stock**

The paper proof is printed on the actual stock the job will use. A screen can't show how ink sits on a specific paper. Coated, uncoated, and textured stocks absorb ink differently, which changes colour, sharpness, and overall appearance. The paper proof shows you what the customer will actually receive.

**Why we do them in this order**

Text corrections are quick and inexpensive to make on screen. A paper proof takes more time and materials to produce. If we found a typo at the paper stage, we would have to fix it and print the proof again. Finalizing the text on screen first means the paper proof only needs to answer one question: does the ink look right on this stock?

---

Small mercy: whoever designed this workflow understood that paper costs money and typos are free until you print them.

**Our Two Proofs: What They Check and Why the Order Matters**

Every job goes through two proofs before it goes to press: a screen proof and then a paper proof. Each one catches a different kind of problem.

**1. Screen proof: checking the words**

The screen proof is where we catch spelling errors. Read every line of text on screen and correct any typos, misspellings, or wrong words before the job moves on.

**Our two print proofs**

Every job goes through two proofs before it's printed: a screen proof, then a paper proof. Each one catches a different kind of problem.

**1. Screen proof: check the words**

The screen proof is where we catch spelling errors. Read every line of text on screen and correct any mistakes before the job moves on.

**2. Paper proof: check the ink on the stock**

The paper proof is printed on the actual stock the job will use. It shows how the ink really looks on that paper, which a screen can't show. Use it to judge how the printed result will look.

Handover Detail

I'm training a new volunteer on our key handover. The outgoing volunteer counts the keys against the register to catch missing keys. The incoming volunteer then signs the register so there's a record of who has custody. Explain what each volunteer does and why, in order.

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 268 words
Two steps, strict order. Swap them and your custody record is fiction.

1. **Outgoing volunteer counts the keys against the register.** They check every key listed is physically present and note anything missing *before* handing off. This catches losses while the person who last held the keys is still standing there to explain them. Once custody transfers, "where's the van key?" becomes a blame-shuffling exercise with no answer.

2. **Incoming volunteer signs the register.** The signature records that they now hold the keys, with a name and time attached. From then on, if a key goes missing, everyone knows who held them and when it happened.

Why that order: the count establishes what is actually being handed over. The signature then records who accepted it. If someone signs first, they've accepted custody of a set nobody has checked.

**f1 Incoming volunteer signs for keys they never counted:** As described, only the outgoing volunteer counts. The new volunteer is putting their name on the outgoing person's word. If a key turns up missing next week, nothing shows whether it was gone at handover or lost afterward. The signature records custody without recording what was in that custody, which is exactly the dispute this process exists to prevent.

**a1 Have both volunteers count together before the signature:** The outgoing volunteer counts aloud, the incoming one watches or recounts, and any discrepancy goes in the register before signing. It adds about a minute, and the signature then confirms an agreed key count rather than blind trust. If you're training someone new anyway, this is the cheapest moment to fix the procedure.
Candidate · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 118 words
Two steps, in this order.

**i1: The outgoing volunteer counts the keys against the register.** This catches missing keys while the person who last held them is still standing there, before anyone else touches the keys.

**i2: The incoming volunteer signs the register.** This records who has custody from this point on, so there's a named person responsible for the keys going forward.

**f1: The order is what makes the handover work.** The count happens before the signature, so the incoming volunteer signs for a set already checked against the register. Reverse the order and the new person ends up signing for a pile of keys nobody has checked, then owning whatever went missing on the previous shift.
Baseline · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 262 words
Two people, one register, and a signature that's meant to turn "someone had the keys" into "this person had the keys."

**Outgoing volunteer: count the keys against the register**
1. Physically count every key and check each one off against the register entry.
2. Flag any discrepancy before handing over.
   - **Why:** It establishes the state of the key set while the outgoing volunteer still has custody. A key missing at this point went missing on their watch, which is the only time anyone can still find out where it went.

**Incoming volunteer: sign the register**
3. Sign the register to accept custody.
   - **Why:** It creates the record of who holds the keys from this moment on. When something disappears next week, you can find the person responsible instead of holding a séance.

**f1 The incoming volunteer signs for keys they never counted:** As you described it, only the outgoing volunteer counts. The incoming volunteer's signature therefore vouches for a count someone else did. Suppose the outgoing person miscounts, or is quietly covering for a missing key. The newcomer signs, inherits the shortfall, and now owns a problem that predates them. That's a poor first lesson for a new volunteer.

**a1 Have both volunteers count together before signing:** The outgoing volunteer counts, the incoming volunteer watches or recounts, and any discrepancy goes in the register before the signature. That way the signature means "I verified this," not "I trust the last person." If your current process already works this way, tell me and I'll stop being paranoid. For about thirty seconds.
Candidate · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 100 words
Two steps, in this order: the outgoing volunteer counts, then the incoming volunteer signs.

**i1** **The outgoing volunteer counts the keys against the register.** This catches missing keys while the person who last had custody is still accountable for them, before responsibility changes hands.

**i2** **The incoming volunteer signs the register.** This records who now has custody of the keys, so there's a written trail of who was responsible and when.

The order matters because signing first means the new volunteer takes custody of whatever happens to be in the box, including the hole where a key used to be.

Handover Detail

I'm training a new volunteer on our key handover. The outgoing volunteer counts the keys against the register to catch missing keys. The incoming volunteer then signs the register so there's a record of who has custody. Explain what each volunteer does and why, in order.

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 214 words

Blind review · Missed
Invents a required reporting step and personal liability, then recommends changing the procedure without a separate action ID.

The handover is a two-step chain of custody: first prove what's there, then record who now owns it.

1. **Outgoing volunteer counts the keys against the register.** They check every key listed is physically present. This catches a missing key while it's still their shift and the trail is warm, rather than letting it quietly become the next person's mystery.
2. **Outgoing volunteer reports any discrepancy before handing over.** A missing key gets noted and escalated now. If it's discovered after the signature, nobody can tell whose watch it went missing on.
3. **Incoming volunteer signs the register.** The signature records that custody has transferred to them. From that point, any key that goes missing is their responsibility to account for.

**f1 Incoming volunteer signs for keys they didn't count.** As described, the incoming person accepts liability based on someone else's count. If the outgoing volunteer miscounts, or is the one who lost the key, the new volunteer has just signed for the hole. The usual fix is to have the incoming volunteer count alongside the outgoing one, or recount before signing. Then the signature means "I verified these," not "I trust whoever was here before me." That costs about two minutes and turns the register into actual evidence instead of a vibes-based ledger.

Our build guide says dependency locking belongs in each service's own build configuration. It also describes generating lockfiles, verification metadata and CI checks. Turn on locking where?

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic miss · 296 words

unrequested detail: verification metadata · unrequested detail: write-locks · length · too many labeled items

Short answer: turn locking on in **each service's own `build.gradle(.kts)`**. Don't put it in a root `allprojects {}`/`subprojects {}` block or a shared convention plugin. I haven't read your guide or this repo yet. I'm assuming Gradle, because "verification metadata" is a Gradle term.

**f1 Locking goes in each service's build script.** This matches what the guide says:

```kotlin
dependencyLocking {
    lockAllConfigurations()
    // lockMode.set(LockMode.STRICT)  // fail if a lockfile is missing, not just mismatched
}
```

Generate the lockfiles per service with `./gradlew :<service>:dependencies --write-locks`. Each one lands as `gradle.lockfile` next to that service's build script. Commit it.

**f2 Verification metadata can't be per-service.** `gradle/verification-metadata.xml` covers the whole build and lives in the root build's `gradle/` directory. Generate it once with `./gradlew --write-verification-metadata sha256 help`. If your services are separate Gradle builds, each with its own `settings.gradle`, then each has its own root and its own file. In a multi-project build there is exactly one. If the guide says to put it next to each service, the guide is wrong.

**f3 Plugin classpath locking is a separate setting.** `dependencyLocking {}` doesn't lock the plugin classpath. If you want plugins pinned too, you need `buildscript { configurations.classpath { resolutionStrategy.activateDependencyLocking() } }` in each service's script, or in `settings.gradle` for settings plugins.

**f4 The CI check doesn't need much.** Once lockfiles and verification metadata are committed, a normal `./gradlew build` already fails on lock drift or checksum mismatch. The only thing to add is `LockMode.STRICT`, so a service that never generated a lockfile can't pass by default.

**a1 Check the guide against the repo.** I can map exactly which services need the block and whether this is one Gradle build or several. That decides whether you get one verification file or several. Say go and I'll read both.

Detail Request

Explain all three recovery steps in order and why each matters. The procedure says to pause writers to stop new changes, copy the journal to preserve a recovery point, then resume writers to restore service. We are documenting the procedure in chat, so I want the reasoning for every step.

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 392 words
Pause, copy, resume is a consistent snapshot dance: freeze the state, capture it, then let the chaos back in.

**l1** — The three steps, in order

- **i1 Pause writers to stop new changes.** A journal being appended while you copy it gives you a torn file: half a record at the end, or a copy that doesn't match any real moment in time. Pausing makes the journal static so the copy is one coherent point. Order matters because copying first and pausing second just stops writes after you've already captured garbage.
- **i2 Copy the journal to preserve a recovery point.** This is the whole point of the procedure. The copy is a known-good state you can replay or roll back to if later recovery work, or the system itself, goes sideways. It has to happen while writers are paused, because that's the only window where "the journal" means one specific thing.
- **i3 Resume writers to restore service.** The pause is an outage, and every second of it costs you something. Resuming ends the impact. It comes last because the moment writers start again, the state you just captured is gone and you can't recapture it.

Three gaps in the procedure as written, if you're documenting it for people who'll run it at 3 a.m.:

**f1 No verification before resume.** Once writers resume (i3), you can't recreate that recovery point. Add a step between copy and resume: checksum or size-compare the copy, or at least confirm the copy completed without errors. Otherwise you could resume service holding a truncated backup and only find out during the incident you needed it for.

**f2 "Pause" needs to mean drain, not just block.** If pausing only rejects new writes, in-flight transactions and buffered data may not be in the journal file yet. The doc should say: stop accepting writes, wait for in-flight writes to commit, flush or fsync, *then* copy.

**f3 The journal alone may not be enough to recover.** Many systems need the data files plus the journal at a consistent point. A journal copy with no matching base state may be unreplayable. Whether this applies depends on the system, so the doc should name what the journal is replayed against.

If you tell me which system this is for, I can tighten the wording to its actual flush and pause semantics.
Candidate · repetition 1 · Automatic pass · 395 words
Each step exists because the next one is worthless without it.

**l1** The three steps and why each matters:

- **l1i1 Pause writers to stop new changes.** The journal has to hold still while you copy it. If writers keep going, the copy captures a file that changes mid-read: the start reflects one moment, the end reflects another, and the last record may be half-written. That gives you a recovery point that looks fine and fails on replay, which is worse than having no backup. "Paused" has to mean in-flight writes are finished and flushed to disk, not just that new requests are being refused. Otherwise the tail of the journal is still in a buffer somewhere and isn't part of what you copy.

- **l1i2 Copy the journal to preserve a recovery point.** This is the whole reason for the procedure. The journal is the ordered record of every change, so a consistent copy of it lets you rebuild state up to the moment of the pause. That holds even if whatever you're recovering from (corruption, a bad migration, a disk dying) gets worse after you resume. The copy has to go somewhere that doesn't share the original's failure mode: a different volume or host, not a sibling directory on the same disk. Before moving on, check that the copy is complete, using a checksum or size match, or ideally a test replay. An unverified copy is a superstition.

- **l1i3 Resume writers to restore service.** Every second writers stay paused is an outage, so this step ends the downtime the first step started. It comes last for a reason: resuming before the copy is verified reopens the window step one closed, and you're back to copying a moving target. Also confirm that writers actually came back. Queued or retried writes from clients that timed out during the pause can arrive as a burst, so watch error rates and lag for a few minutes before calling it done.

**f1 The pause should be kept short.** The pause is the only part of this procedure users can feel, so its length is set by how fast the copy runs. If the journal is large, a filesystem or volume snapshot followed by an immediate resume, with the copy taken from the snapshot afterward, gets you the same consistency guarantee with seconds of downtime instead of minutes.
Baseline · repetition 2 · Automatic pass · 335 words
The procedure is simple: freeze the data, copy it, unfreeze it. Each step only works if the step before it actually finished.

**l1 Recovery steps, in order**

- **i1 Pause writers to stop new changes.** A copy is only useful if the journal stops changing while you read it. If writers keep going, you get a file that's half before and half after, which no replay tool can make sense of. Pausing gives you a fixed cutoff point. "Paused" has to mean writes in flight have finished and been flushed to disk, not just that new requests are being turned away. Otherwise you're copying a journal with its last records missing.
- **i2 Copy the journal to preserve a recovery point.** The journal is the ordered record of every change. With it you can rebuild state up to the cutoff, or replay it into a fresh instance if the primary dies. Copying it while frozen gives you a consistent snapshot that won't change underneath you. It's your undo button if resuming goes wrong. It's also evidence if you need to work out what broke in the first place.
- **i3 Resume writers to restore service.** Every second writers stay paused is downtime for anything that writes. Resuming is the whole reason to keep steps 1 and 2 short. The order matters: resume only after the copy is complete. Resume early and you're back to copying a moving target.

**f1 Unverified copy before resume:** As written, the procedure resumes writers without checking that the copy actually worked. If the copy is truncated or corrupt, you lose the recovery point at the exact moment you've thrown away the clean state, and you won't find out until you try to restore from it. Add a check between steps 2 and 3:

- compare the copy's size or checksum against the source
- confirm the last record in the copy matches the cutoff you paused at

It costs seconds of extra downtime and saves you a restore from garbage.

Our build guide says dependency locking belongs in each service's own build configuration. It also describes generating lockfiles, verification metadata and CI checks. Turn on locking where?

Baseline · repetition 1 · Automatic miss · 307 words

unrequested detail: verification metadata · unrequested detail: write-locks · length · too many labeled items

In each service's own build file. The guide is right about locking, but not everything it lists is per-service. "Verification metadata" means Gradle, so this assumes Gradle. I haven't opened your guide or build files, so check this against the actual tree before believing me any more than you believe the guide.

**f1** **Dependency locking is turned on per service.** Put this in every service's `build.gradle(.kts)`:

```kotlin
dependencyLocking {
    lockAllConfigurations()
    lockMode = LockMode.STRICT   // fail if a lockfile is missing, not just stale
}
```

Each service then gets its own `gradle.lockfile` next to its build file. To generate it, run a task that resolves every configuration with `--write-locks`, for example `./gradlew :svc:dependencies --write-locks`. If you only run `build`, configurations that never resolve stay unlocked, and nobody notices until something drifts in production.

**f2** **Verification metadata is not per service.** There is exactly one file for the whole build: `gradle/verification-metadata.xml` at the root. Generate it with `./gradlew --write-verification-metadata sha256 <task that resolves everything>`. If the guide puts this under "each service," that part is wrong. Separate copies per service either get ignored or rot independently.

**f3** **Build-script plugins are not locked by the snippet in f1.** That block covers the service's own dependencies only. To lock plugins too, add `buildscript { configurations.classpath { resolutionStrategy.activateDependencyLocking() } }` in the service. That produces `buildscript-gradle.lockfile`. Settings plugins get the same treatment in `settings.gradle(.kts)`, producing `settings-gradle.lockfile`. Without this, your shiny lockfiles still let a plugin update pull in whatever it likes.

**f4** **CI checks.** Two jobs:
- **f4i1** Run the normal build with nothing disabled. Lock mismatches and checksum failures already fail the build. Make sure nobody has slipped in `--dependency-verification=off` or `lenient`.
- **f4i2** Run a drift job: regenerate the locks and the metadata, then `git diff --exit-code`. This catches people who changed dependencies and "forgot" to commit the lockfile.
